| ✅ | **Three item sizes in the ribbon band, learned from Word by driving it** — 2026-08-24, O31. Word's ribbon rules are in no API, so `tools/word-ribbon-study.ps1` photographed it at twelve widths and `tools/our-ribbon-study.ps1` photographed ours at the same ones. **The measurement: at 884 client points Word put ten groups on the band and this shell put three.** Word's density comes from mixing **Large** (icon above label, spanning the rows), **Medium** (icon + label) and **Small** (icon only) within one group; every control here was Medium by a hard-coded `shows_label: true`. Now declared per item in the manifest, defaulting to Medium so a manifest that says nothing renders identically. ★★ **Small is earned** — icon, tooltip *and* an installed painter, or it falls back to labelled; the tooltip is the icon's accessible name, and twelve of 109 commands have no icon and needed no audit. ★★★ **The File tab is deliberately unchanged**: its commands are *named things* ("Export form data…"), not iconic ones, and `band.rs`'s original argument was right about exactly that case — driving Word showed the argument is about the **command**, not the band. `Large` is applied only to one-item groups, because Large items lead their group and reordering a multi-item group is `RIBBON_IA.md`'s call, not this change's |
| ✅ | **An item can be hidden by condition, and its space is reclaimed** — 2026-08-24, O31's second half. `visible_when` on a ribbon or menu item, evaluated against the same `ConditionSet` that decides enablement, and applied **before measurement**: the group re-flows, and a group with nothing left is not drawn at all, separator included. ★ This is **visibility**, not enablement, and R9 draws the line — `Command::enable` is the greying, this is the disappearing. It is the mechanism that will let one tab definition serve Read, Review and Edit rather than three near-identical tabs; **which commands should differ between the modes is the operator's call and is not yet made** |
| ✅ | **A search that finds nothing now says whether it could have** — 2026-08-25, against `pdfcer-core` v0.11.0's `search_text`. ★★★ A zero-result search has **two** causes that look identical: the word is not in the document, or *the document's text was never recoverable as letters, so no word could ever have matched*. The second does not look broken — the text **renders perfectly**, which is exactly what makes it invisible. Type 3 fonts and `Identity-H` fonts with no `/ToUnicode` map are the two populations, and the engine now counts them. Find no longer answers those with a bare "No matches": it says how many fonts in the document store text nothing can search, with a hover explaining that such text prints correctly and that recognising the page adds a searchable layer. ★ **Acrobat has the identical limit** — its search and copy pipeline for Type 3 is gated on the same entry — and answers it by giving up silently. Saying so is rule 4's less-remembered half: an inference the operator *cannot see* still owes them a report. ★★ Off-canvas, per decision 059: it appears in the Find bar's own second row, never as a mark on the page — a badge over the offending text would be a second rendering path for content that is not in doubt |
| ✅ | **Sections re-wrap onto a third row as you narrow the window, before anything is hidden** — 2026-08-25, S5. ★★★ The band's response to running out of width is now a **four-rung ladder**, and the ordering is the design: natural layout → **re-wrap onto up to three rows** → collapse whole groups to a captioned button → scroll the band. Each rung hides strictly more than the one before, so the re-wrap rung is exhausted completely before a single group collapses; five re-wraps beat one collapse however the arithmetic comes out. Measured on the File tab: the Export group is **2 rows at 2200 pt and 3 rows at 1200 pt**, with nothing compacted at all while there is room. ★★ And **the band's height never moves** — the group box is `47 .. 140` at both widths and the document tabs begin at 144 in both. That is not a bigger constant: three rows of full-height controls are half again as tall as two, which would move the canvas under a fit-to-page zoom on every tab click. Word's rows are not uniform either — its tall row carries the combo boxes and its icon rows are shorter — so a re-wrapped group here divides the **same** row area into three. The feature turns itself off rather than clipping: with the shipped theme a compressed row is 16.7 pt against a 16 pt icon, and a theme whose numbers did not clear would simply never re-wrap |
| ✅ | **The ribbon scrolls sideways instead of hiding commands in a menu** — 2026-08-25, S4, on the operator's instruction (*"do the scroll like Word"*, asked twice). A `›` at the band's right edge shifts the band; a `‹` appears once it has been shifted. The `⏷ N more` dropdown is **gone**, not supplemented — two affordances for one job is a defect. ★ The trade is stated rather than glossed: a menu *names* what is hidden and an arrow does not. What the arrow gives back is that a group never changes its nature — never simultaneously "on the ribbon" and "in a menu" — and it is the convention across the product class. ★★ The position is remembered per tab and **re-clamped from scratch every frame**: a stored scroll index is an *input* to layout, so a stale one left behind by a widened window would show blank space with nothing to press. The clamp is a pure function of the width and never reads what was drawn, which is the one thing that keeps this from being the measurement-feeds-its-own-size loop that has cost this project three separate defects |
| ✅ | **…and a tab with nothing left to show is not shown either** — 2026-08-25, the symmetric completion of the rule above. Hiding every item on a tab used to leave an operator a tab they could click with an empty band beneath it. ★★★ That asymmetry is not merely untidy: it is what makes a **generous tab list** usable, and therefore what makes the whole mechanism worth having. A mode can now name a tab it only sometimes needs, hide the items that do not apply, and the tab appears exactly when it has something to offer — turning `Mode::tabs` from *"which tabs exist here"* into *"which tabs may appear here"*. Two guards, both tested: a tab whose items carry **no** conditions is never affected, so a manifest written before conditions existed cannot be hidden by this; and an **empty** tab is still shown, because that is a manifest under construction and hiding it would make a half-written one look like a working one with a feature missing |
| ✅ | **Overprint in print-ready files is now a setting you can reach** — 2026-08-25. The engine's `page_blend_space_source` arrived with Pass 122.5 and had no control, so it was changeable only by hand-editing `settings.txt`, which is not a user interface — pdfcer-gui's own coverage gate caught it. ★ Filed in **Colour**, by the symptom that sends somebody looking: *"the overprinted areas in my print file look wrong"* is an ink question, not an image one. The copy is worded as that symptom rather than as the mechanism — the title is *Overprint in print-ready files*, not *page group blending colour space* — and the three options run **strict → shipped → most literal reading**, so the conforming-but-degenerate answer is met first and *why is the default not what the standard says* is answered before it is asked. The engine measured the stake against the industry print-conformance suite: **24 of its 51 patches request overprint**, and under the literal reading every one is wrong |
| ✅ | **★ Grey over a spot colour got its own setting — 2026-08-28.** The engine's `Pass 143.0` added a second overprint axis and this shell's own coverage gate caught it within one dependency update, which is the mechanism doing exactly what it was built for. A grey fill that overprints a spot colour either knocks the spot out or lets it show through, and the standard genuinely does not say which: §8.6.7 scopes the rule to CMYK, and Acrobat converts grey to black-ink CMYK **first** and then applies it. Both readings are defensible; the default follows Acrobat, because the measuring instrument is scored against Acrobat. ★★ It sits **directly under** the overprint setting above, and the order is an argument: that one decides whether overprint is simulated at all, and this one decides which colours the ink rules then reach — reversed, the window would ask about grey-over-spot before saying whether overprint is drawn. ★ The third option extends the rule to red-green-blue as well, and its note says **nobody has checked it against Acrobat** in those words, because a radio group that presents a guess and a measurement in the same voice asks the operator to trust both equally |
| ✅ | **★★★ A fit now survives a window resize, and a pan gets you out of it — 2026-08-28.** Press **Fit page**, **Fit width** or **Fit height** and then resize the window or drag a dock panel: the page re-fits *and* stays centred. It used to re-scale correctly and stay anchored wherever it happened to sit — the size right, the position stale. ★★★ **And a pan now leaves the fit**, so the position you chose survives the next resize: *"unless the person has changed the zoom or panned around"* — changing the zoom always did leave it; panning did not, and until a resize re-placed anything that was harmless. The two changes only work together. ★★ **The wheel deliberately does not leave the fit**, because scrolling a fit-width document is how every reader in the class is read — a wheel notch that dropped the fit would stop the page re-fitting the moment you looked at the second half of it. Two driven checks assert *opposite* outcomes for the two gestures, so a build that treats all view movement alike fails one of them whichever way it goes. ★★★ **The obvious implementation is wrong and was caught by a test's setup step**: *"a fit is a mode, so re-place every frame"* pins the wheel to the page's origin under Fit page and makes a continuous document unscrollable. The check that scrolls into the pasteboard before pressing anything refused to proceed — *"the pan did not move the page"* — which is a precondition doing its job. The fix keys on the **viewport changing**, which is what the request literally said |
| ✅ | **★★★ Inkscape-style scale switches — 2026-08-28, on *"Inkscape has options for this and I want the same."*** Three checkboxes on the Tool row, live whenever the Select tool is armed: **Scale line weight**, **Keep the inner margins the same size**, and **Allow the artwork to distort**. All three ship off, which is the same default Acrobat, Illustrator and Inkscape all use — a line weight on a drawing is a drafting standard, not decoration. ★★★ **They replaced a derivation, and that is the real change.** The shell was taking *scale the line weight* from whether your drag happened to be proportional, which would have silently overridden your answer on exactly the resizes where you were most likely to have one. ★★ **And the refusal it was working around now speaks.** When pdfcer did not draw a shape, its border can't always keep its weight while the shape grows — no PDF can describe a border that is thicker on one axis, which is a limit of the format and not of pdfcer (Inkscape hit the same wall in SVG and closed it as correct behaviour, then made the border go oval anyway). pdfcer refuses instead, and **says which switch fixes it** — a different sentence for a proportional drag than for a stretched one, because only one switch helps in each case. ★ The margins switch has no Inkscape equivalent and is the opposite default for a reason that unifies both: *is the property a length in the space being scaled?* A margin is; a line weight is not |
| ✅ | **★★ Edit ▸ Objects does something now — 2026-08-28, and it was drawn and inert for the whole life of the project.** The third button in Edit ▸ Content, beside *Edit text* and *Add text*, both of which work. ★★★ **Its tooltip already described a shipped feature**: *"click to select, drag to move the object, drag an anchor to move that node, or press Delete to remove it"* — every clause of that landed in Phase 1. It is the Select tool, described exactly, by a control that did nothing. ★★ It is the **last inert command with no recorded reason**, and its register entry admitted as much in those words — *"NO RECORDED REASON ANYWHERE … inferring a deferral is not the same as recording one"*. Honest, correct, and it survived three sessions unchallenged, because **an entry confessing to have no reason reads like the output of a search that already happened**. It is indistinguishable from a deliberate deferral somebody forgot to explain, and only one of those two invites a re-derivation. ⇒ The rule the audit ends on: *"no reason recorded"* is the **first** entry to re-check, not the last. Four inert commands remain, two of them drawn |
| ✅ | **★★★ Right-click a form field and you get the field's menu — 2026-08-28.** Until today a right-click on a text box offered **four zoom levels**: the canvas had exactly two questions (*is an object under the pointer* / *is anything*), and a form field is neither — it is not in the object selection at all, by design. So the one gesture every editor answers with a menu about the thing you pointed at answered with a menu about the view. ★★ **Properties and Delete**, in that order, destructive last. Rename is deliberately absent and goes through Properties: renaming needs typing, a menu cannot ask for text, and a second half-built rename that could disagree with the panel's is worse than one click further. ★★★ **Delete-the-key and Delete-the-command were acting on different things**, and this is what exposed it: the key had reached a selected field since this morning and the command had not, so the new menu's Delete would have done nothing. One rule now, read from one place. ★ And the menu is decided by a **hit test, not by what is selected** — the selection a right-click creates is applied at the end of the frame while the menu opens *on* the click, so a version that read the selection would have shown the previously-selected field's menu, permanently |
| ✅ | **★★★ The first driven context menu in this project's history — and the harness had no right-click at all.** 92 driven checks, canvas menus since Phase 1, and **not one of them had ever opened a menu**: everything asserted about them asked whether the manifest *would* offer something, which is not the same question as whether a right-click on a pixel opens anything. There was no `right_click_at` to call. ⇒ **A gesture with no driver is a gesture R1 cannot reach, and the gap leaves no failing test behind to advertise itself** — it surfaced only because a fourth menu was added and somebody went looking. The check asserts two things, and the second is the one that matters: the menu resolved *and* it had something in it. For ten minutes it had nothing — both items were gated on a condition that is false while a field is selected, so the popup would never have appeared and a one-assertion check would have passed |
| ✅ | **★★ The comments you write are signed and dated — 2026-08-28.** Every sticky note, text box and stamp pdfcer authored before today was **anonymous and undated**: `/T` and `/M` were never written, so the author column in Acrobat's comment list and in pdfcer's own Comments panel was blank on everything this program made. A comment nobody signed is a comment nobody can answer. Settings ▸ **Comments ▸ Your name** fills it, and **blank is a supported answer** — it leaves comments anonymous, which is legal and is what pdfcer did until today. ★★★ **pdfcer does not take your Windows login for this**, and the option note says so: the name goes into files you send to other people, so it is yours to choose rather than something the program reads off your account. ★★ The date is **UTC**, and the reasoning is written down because the tempting answer is the wrong one: local time labelled `Z` is the string that looks right to whoever typed the comment and is a lie in the file that nobody would notice until two reviewers in two countries compared notes. `pdfcer-core` deliberately refuses to read a clock at all — a library that does cannot be tested by comparing bytes, and a timestamp is a *claim* — so the obligation lands here, in one module, whose test asserts the engine accepts what it writes. ★ **What is still missing and is filed**: a note on a *shape*. A cloud round a mistake, a comment on a highlight, a correction to a comment written a minute ago — all need a verb that sets a note on an annotation that **already exists**, and `MarkupOptions` is author-time only. Until then the Comments panel lists comments and cannot write one |
| ✅ | **★★★ A page's colours no longer change with the zoom — 2026-08-26**, on his report: *"seems I get different results depending on Zoom level … up to 474 % they are mismatched, but at 579 % they match."* A page that declares a subtractive blending space is composited in a four-colorant buffer at 20 B/px; past a ceiling the engine refuses it, composites in sRGB and says so, and the patches move by up to **16 levels of 255**. That ceiling is **3.76× lower than `MAX_PIXMAP_EDGE`** — 518 % against 1946 % on real A4 — so every whole-page raster in the band came back approximate. `render::strategy::for_page` now ends the whole-page tier at whichever ceiling bites first, and a **region** raster stays under the colour one at any zoom because its buffer is sized to the region rather than to the page. ★★★ **And it is OBSERVED, not assumed, which is the entire design.** Applying the ceiling unconditionally would have taken free panning away from the operator at **263 % zoom on his own D-size drawing sheet** — a sheet with no transparency on it, so nothing would have been gained; about **0.4 %** of real files use the buffer at all (15 of 4,012 in the engine's corpus). So `OpenDoc::ink_pages` records which pages have been *seen* compositing in ink, from the renderer's own counters, written in one place and traced as `ink-page`; only those get the lower ceiling. ★ Beside it, **Settings ▸ Colour ▸ "Colours changing when you zoom"** — the window's only free-text control, uncapped on his ruling, parsed and shown with the engine's own `parse_byte_size`/`format_byte_size` so the window and `settings.txt` accept identical strings. The engine made the ceiling readable and settable **at this shell's request**, in v0.14.0, the same afternoon it was filed. **Driven:** at 801 % the trace reads `cmyk_buffer=true refused=0` where it read `refused=1` before, and every assertion was falsified by disabling the mechanism |
| ✅ | **★★ The chrome survives a narrow window and a large UI scale — 2026-08-26.** Found by the first driven run in which every check launched. At `ui_scale = 1.80` the zoom control, the fit buttons, Find and the selection filter were **off the left edge at negative coordinates**, with the left-hand notes drawn underneath the fit group; and the bar was two points shorter than its own controls at *every* scale, because its height was a constant written for 24-point controls while the shipped theme's are 28. The bar now **sheds** as it narrows — biggest and least essential first, the way Word's and VS Code's do — and its height comes from the theme. ★★★ The clause that makes shedding legitimate is **enforced against the real command registry**, and it refused the obvious design outright: shedding from the low-priority end means shedding the leftmost, which is the selection filter, and the filter has **no ribbon command, no menu entry and no shortcut** — it exists only on that bar. Only the fit group and Find may go, and dropping the fit group alone (298 pt of a 611 pt bar) is enough. ★ The existing height test asserts the exact property and **passes against the broken build**, because it measures in a bare `egui::Context` with no theme in it — R1's founding shape, verbatim, so a second test applies every shipped preset |
| ✅ | **★★★ The Properties panel scrolls, and its Apply button can be reached — 2026-08-26.** Reported as *"typing a width and pressing Apply does nothing"*; Apply had **never been pressed by anybody**. The panel drew its sections straight into the dock tile with **no scroll area around them** — only the read-only metadata rows at the very bottom had one, nested deep inside — so with an object selected you got Left, Bottom, Width and Height, and Apply sat below the window edge with no scrollbar and no gesture that would reach it. The typed-geometry feature was complete, wired, tested and **unusable**. One scroll area round the whole body, the nested one removed. ★ It took a **screenshot** to see: the coordinates said so all along — `properties.geometry.apply` at y 776 in a viewport ending at 762 — and three readings of them still reached the wrong conclusion, which is the standing rule about layout defects having exactly one oracle. The panel's regions now publish through `ui_rect_visible`, so a control nobody can see is not offered to a harness as a target |
| ✅ | **Two-row ribbon bands** — 2026-08-14, operator feedback: *"your ribbons are only one row. the mockup previews I was shown have two rows and seem less cluttered."* `mockups/ribbon.html` specifies it precisely — `.gcmds {flex-wrap:wrap; max-width:440px}` inside a `.band {min-height:86px}` — and the shipped `render_group` emitted a single `ui.horizontal`, so controls never wrapped. Now `GROUP_ROWS = 2` with a balancing wrap that minimises the *widest* row rather than filling greedily as the mockup does. **Measured at 1,100 px, one variable changed**: Markup overflow 3 groups → **2**, File 3 → **1**, the Shapes group **45 % narrower** (548.7 → 300.5 pt). The row count is a **constant, not a parameter** — per-group or per-tab would make the band content-driven again, which is R128 exactly; a constant makes *"same height on every tab"* true by construction, and that is asserted including for the case where every group is in the overflow menu. Three rows refused: another `control_height + gutter` off the canvas permanently, and a caption three rows below its top row stops reading as that row's label. **Icons untouched** — the mockup had none and was still preferred, so the staging was the request |
| ✅ | **…and the group padding the mockup specifies is now drawn** — controls sat *flush* against their group box and against the separators, measured at an inset of **0.0**. Now 6 pt each side, symmetric, plus 4 pt of band bottom padding so captions no longer sit on the seam with the dock below. **It cost zero width budget**: `plan::GROUP_PADDING` had been budgeting 6 pt/side that the renderer never drew, and its own doc comment said so — the planner was reserving space the renderer spent as an accidental margin. Overflow unchanged on both tabs, verified rather than assumed. ★ **The mockup's 13 px and the build's 6 pt are the same gap**: the mockup separates groups with a zero-width `border-right` (13+0+13 = 26) and the build with a real 14 pt `ui.separator()` (6+14+6 = 26, measured at exactly 26.0). Raising the constant to 13 would have made it 40 pt and cost 70 pt across File's five band groups — enough to push a group out, which is the regression the change existed to avoid |
| ⬜ | **…and the remaining overflow is not a layout problem** — Style and Comments still overflow at 1,100 px and **no row count can fix them**: they are one-item groups (a custom colour swatch; one command), so they have nothing to wrap. That is the Markup-tab taxonomy question — does Finish belong in Shapes, does Ink, is Shapes now two bands — and it is the operator's, not a defect |

### OCR — specified and unblocked by the operator, 2026-08-14

✅ **The licensing question is answered.** Operator, 2026-08-14: *"yes ship
that model in the mit repo with proper credit."* The **mechanism to carry that
credit now exists** — see the attribution row below — and what remains when
OCR lands is one line in `PAYLOAD_ASSET_DIRS`, one section copied from the
engine's `about.hbs`, and one `Attribution` entry. **The gate refuses the
first without the other two**, which is the property that makes "with proper
credit" enforced rather than remembered.
The engine can already recognise text end to end (`ocr::layer` writes the
invisible sandwich at render mode 3, the `ocrs` weights ship at 12,240,008 B)
and **no shell has a surface**. The engine also records that recognition
quality is **unproven**: its only test documents are vector PDFs that already
contain text. Settle both before building.

**The behaviour is now specified, so it does not have to be re-derived when
the blocker lifts:**

| | |
|---|---|
| ✅ | **Find offers OCR when there is no text to find** — the trigger is `readout == Empty` **AND** `!page_has_extractable_text()`, the second behind a short-circuit so it is never evaluated unless a search already came back empty. The falsifying test is the one that matters: `an_ordinary_empty_result_on_a_text_page_offers_nothing`. A build that offered on *any* zero-hit search is one character simpler, passes the positive test perfectly, and would put *"this page has no text on it"* under every mistyped part number |
| ✅ | **★★★ OCR IS AN EDIT TO THE DOCUMENT YOU HAVE OPEN — 2026-08-27.** Operator: *"Why do I have to save a copy instead of just go back into my pdf and save over it or save from there?"* He was right, and the cause was structural rather than a choice this shell made: `ocr::layer::add_ocr_layer` took an immutable `&Document` and returned a whole new PDF, so recognition was the one capability in pdfcer that was not an edit and a shell holding an open session had nothing to put the layer *into*. Filed on the request channel 2026-08-26; `EditSession::add_ocr_layer` shipped 2026-08-27 (engine Pass 135.0). The layer now lands in the session, **`Ctrl+S` writes it and `Ctrl+Z` takes it back out**, and a forty-page run is **one** undo entry rather than forty. What went with it: a Save-as picker, a suggested filename, an in-place file writer, a document reloader, four copy strings and the `PDFCER_DIAG_SAVE_PATH` harness seam. ★★ **And the unsaved-edits refusal is gone, which no guard could ever have fixed.** The free function read the document's *base* revision, so a recognised copy taken after an edit silently omitted it — refusing was correct, because silent omission is worse. But a session never becomes clean again, not even after a save, so **OCR died for the rest of the session the first time anything was edited** and reported `UnsavedEdits` on the way out, which by then was false. The session verb plans against the session graph, so the divergence is removed rather than policed. Driven end to end: `ocr-recognised pages=1 recognised=97` → `ocr-applied written=1` → `ocr-layer page=0 n=1 epoch=1`, and the file on disk **byte-identical afterwards** — because nobody saved, which is where an unsaved edit belongs |
| ✅ | **★★ Recognise more than one page — All pages, this page, or a typed range.** Operator, three ways in one message: *"how do I OCR more than one page? Why does the tool stop at one? […] Where is the option to select more than one page?"* There was none, and no engine limitation required that. **All pages is first and is the default**, which is what every surveyed recogniser does; the old behaviour is the second radio. The range field parses through `dialogs::print::tabs::parse_page_range` — the **print dialog's own parser**, deliberately, because two page-range parsers in one program would accept different things on two surfaces and the operator would have to learn which one they were talking to; a test asserts the two agree rather than merely resemble each other. An unresolvable range greys the Recognise button and says why, rather than colouring the field red: a half-typed `1-` is an ordinary state of a text field somebody is using. ★ **The models are loaded once per run, not once per page** — `OcrsEngine::from_model_dir` used to sit inside the per-page path, which was invisible while the dialog could only do one page and is the difference between a fifty-page run an operator waits through and one they abandon |
| ✅ | **★★ A page that already has text is skipped by default, and that is a hazard guard rather than a preference** — measured 2026-08-26: a second pass over a recognised page took it from **427 character codes to 854**. `add_ocr_layer` *adds* a layer, it does not replace one, and the layer is invisible (Table 106 mode 3), so nothing on screen changes and nothing warns — what changes is that every Find hit is doubled and every copy comes out twice. The failure is silent, cumulative, and produced by the operator doing the reasonable thing of running the recogniser again over a document they were not sure about. Per **page**, never per run: on a scanned drawing bound with a typed cover sheet the cover is skipped and the scan is recognised. `Refusal::AlreadyHasText` is a distinct refusal from `NothingRecognised` and says a different sentence — *it declined to look* against *it looked and found nothing* — because the two have different remedies |
| ✅ | **OCR in Read mode** — `file.ocr` is reachable in Read, and the driven check asserts that specifically. It used to be a special case of *"Read may produce a new document; it may not modify this one"*, enforced at the save because OCR's bytes had nowhere to go but a new file. That is no longer the mechanism: recognition is an edit, so what governs it in Read is whatever governs every other edit in Read, which is the mode's own capability set. **The rule did not weaken; it stopped being restated in a second place** |
| ✅ | **…and it lives on File ▸ Recognise, not Tools ▸ Recognise** — `RIBBON_IA.md` §5.7 specified Tools, and Tools is not in Read's tab list, so building to the specification would have broken the instruction. **Third instance of one pattern**, now a rule in §5.7: a command refused where the operator needs it means the *tab* is wrong. Giving Read the Tools tab was the alternative and was refused — it would hand a reading stance batch merge, split and font embedding |
| ✅ | **★ RE-MEASURED 2026-08-26: more scanning resolution makes OCR worse, and 300 DPI is the worst of five** — the conventional answer, and the one this module first shipped with. On the benchmark CAD sheet, against its own vector text as ground truth: 72 → 56.5 %, **100 → 56.7 %**, 150 → 54.5 %, 200 → 53.9 %, 300 → **35.1 %**. Cause unchanged: `ocrs` resizes every image to its model's fixed input, so **pixel count governs, not resolution** — past a point, more pixels only means more downscaling before the model sees them. ★★ These numbers **replace a retracted set** measured against a text-detection model that had never worked (Pass 129.0); the detector was verified by hash before re-measuring. What survived the retraction is the headline — more is worse, 300 is worst — now confirmed twice by two different detectors. What did **not** survive is the sharp peak at 150: the real curve is a **plateau from 72 to 200** (a spread under three points, inside the noise of a 130-token sample) and then a cliff. ★ `TARGET_PIXELS = 8_400_000` therefore **does not move** — it puts the benchmark at 150 DPI, inside the plateau. It was right for a wrong reason and is now right for a measured one. Still two CAD documents, not a corpus of real scans: on ordinary material the engine reads a blurred, skewed, noisy synthetic scan at **47 of 47 words**, so these figures are the hard end rather than the typical one |
| ✅ | **★★★ A run says how far it has got, and Stop and Cancel mean different things — DRIVEN 2026-09-01 on his own scanned parts manual.** His words: *"give feedback on what it is doing when it is running (pages done, words/characters detected) so that the user can see that it is doing something and hasn't frozen on large documents. Maybe a cancel and stop button too. The cancel throws away what was done, and the stop finished the page it is on and keeps the work it has done."* Built exactly so — **Stop** finishes the page in flight and keeps everything, **Cancel** discards, and where both are pressed **Cancel wins in either order**. ★★ It had been built and **never driven**, which under R1 is not shipped, and it needed a scan with no text layer that nobody had. He named one this session: an **883-page, 266 MB parts manual, every page an image, `/Rotate 270`, not one extractable character in it** — measured at **2.6 s and ~440 recognised words a page**, so the whole manual is about 38 minutes. Eight pages of it now back three driven checks, run twice each: on his scan the tally moved through 7 values reporting **2,218 words / 12,431 characters**, Stop ended at 2 of 8 and the session kept 758 words, Cancel left **no** `ocr-applied` and **no** `ocr-layer` anywhere in the run. ★★ **Falsified, not merely passed** — `job.stop()` replaced with `job.cancel()` and the Stop check failed naming the cause; restored and green. ★★★ **Two things the driving found that no unit test could.** A rect is not an oracle for *"the user can see it is working"*: a label frozen at `Page 1 of 8` declares the same rect while being the stalled program he is afraid of, so the shell now traces the **numbers** and the check asserts they move. And **live progress was resting on a decorative widget** — egui is immediate-mode and idle, the worker is on another thread and raises no input events, so nothing was requesting the next frame; it worked only because `egui::Spinner` calls `request_repaint()` for its own animation. Swapping the spinner for a progress bar would have silently taken live progress with it and left every test green. The dialog now asks for the repaint itself and says why. ★ One measured behaviour that is **correct** and looks like a defect: the tally ends at **7 of 8**, never 8 — `Job::poll` drains the channel, so the frame that reads the last page's report is the frame that reads `Finished` and the dialog leaves the working phase before drawing it. The outcome then states the true totals |
| ⚠ | **Recognition quality on real scans is still unproven, and it is a NARROWER gap than it was** — ★ updated 2026-09-01. A genuinely scanned document finally exists on this machine: an **883-page, image-only parts manual** at `OneDrive\pdfTests\Parts Manual TH83 Telehandler.pdf`, `/Rotate 270` throughout, on which the recogniser returns **~440 words a page at 2.6 s a page** and the OCRed text extracts and is selectable. So the pipeline is now known to work on real scanned material, which it was not before. ★★ **What is still unmeasured is ACCURACY**, and the reason is that there is no ground truth: nobody has typed out what those pages say, so *"440 words came back"* is not *"440 words came back right"*. The two CAD-sheet accuracy measurements below remain the only scored ones, and they are of vector text rasterized on purpose rather than of a scan. It is also **not committed** — 266 MB — so the tree still has no scanned PDF in it and the driven checks fall back to a synthetic fixture unless `$PDFCER_VERIFY_SCAN` names one. The original caveat, for the fixture that is committed:  30 documents in `D:\Dev	emp\pdfcer\` and one fixture, every one carrying text-showing operators. `fixtures/synthetic-image-only.pdf` is a raster with one `Do` and no text operator; it establishes **the plumbing** — detection of no-text, the Find offer, the recogniser running, the invisible layer at render mode 3, the text becoming findable — and **nothing about quality**, having no noise, skew, ringing or uneven lighting. That caveat is in the fixture's header, in the check's report lines, and here |
| ✅ | **What the operator is told about confidence** — `ocrs` scores nothing, so the dialog recognises and **discloses**. ★ The disclosure used to sit between recognition and a save the operator could decline, which gave it force; as of 2026-08-27 the layer is already in the document when it is read, and the force it has now is **`Ctrl+Z`** — named in the outcome sentence for that reason. The engine's per-page counts go out on the ordinary edit-disclosure channel with every other edit's rather than being re-rendered on this window: two accounts of one run, worded differently, is a pair that drifts. One shell-owned sentence sits above the engine's own list: *"This recogniser reports no confidence score for any word, so nothing here has been checked — that is not the same as everything being right."* Tests forbid accuracy claims anywhere on the surface |

★ **Two observations on that second row, both of which change what has to be
built rather than merely how it is described.**

**First, it is currently vacuous — and that is a fact about today, not a
reason to ignore it.** This shell has exactly one save command,
`file.save_copy` ("Save a copy…"), and `text::commands`' own note explains
that the label is load-bearing: pdfcer *never* overwrites the original unless
the operator picks it, because a button labelled `Save` would promise
in-place saving, "which cannot ship before autosave and crash recovery
exist." So every mode already behaves the way the instruction asks Read to
behave. **The rule becomes load-bearing the day in-place `Save` lands**, and
that is the day it must be honoured — which is why it is written down now,
against a command that does not yet exist.

**Second, the instruction generalises, and the general form is the better
rule.** Stated as *"OCR prompts Save as in Read"* it is one feature's special
case. Stated as **"Read may produce a new document; it may not modify this
one"** it covers OCR, and it also settles in advance every future capability
of the same shape — flatten, redact-apply, PDF/A convert, page export. It
sits alongside the two exceptions Read already carries (**form filling**, and
now **OCR**), and it explains why both are exceptions rather than
inconsistencies: neither changes the document the operator was given.

| ✅ | **An attribution surface for shipped assets** — 2026-08-14, built as OCR's prerequisite. `PROVENANCE.md` per asset directory, `about.toml`/`about.hbs` generating a **319,430-byte `THIRD_PARTY_LICENSES.md`** into the package, a **File ▸ About** dialog (`file.about`), and a `check-shipped-assets` gate ported from the engine with a **seven-check** self-test. Two surfaces because a GUI needs both: the file carries the licence *texts*, the dialog carries the *attribution*. CC-BY-SA-4.0's **BY** clause requires the notice reach the end user, not merely a reader of the source tree — that distinction is the engine checker's own reasoning and it is why a `PROVENANCE.md` alone was never enough |
| ⚠ | **★ …and building it found three third-party works already being redistributed with no notice at all** — a **pre-existing** gap, not one OCR introduced: the **14 Foxit CFF faces** (BSD-3-Clause, `include_bytes!` in `pdfcer-render`), the **Adobe Core-14 AFM metrics** (APAFML) and the **Adobe Glyph List** (BSD-3-Clause), all compiled into the binary. They now appear in the notice and the About dialog. Worth stating plainly: the shell has been shipping them unattributed for its whole life, and nothing detected it until an unrelated feature required the mechanism that could |

| ✅ | **★ Save a copy — wired 2026-08-14, and it had been inert for the whole project.** `file.save_copy` was registered, on the **quick access toolbar**, bound to **Ctrl+S**, and printed the chord in its own tooltip — with **no dispatch arm**. Nothing this shell built could be written to disk: not a dimension, not a markup, not a form fill, not a newly created document. It writes an **incremental** update via `to_incremental_bytes`, which is what the shipped tooltip already promised (*"the edits are appended as an update so the previous version stays intact inside the file"*), so the copy begins with the original **byte for byte** and a test asserts exactly that. Neither `edit_epoch` nor `save_pending()` moves — saving a copy changes nothing about the open document, and zeroing the epoch would silently switch off the OCR preflight's named refusal. **Proven by a second process** opening the saved file and counting one more annotation than the fixture had |
| ✅ | **New PDF** — `file.new`, `Ctrl+N`. The engine has **no document-creation verb** and `document.rs` carries a named permanent invariant forbidding one (*"no separate builder/generation model may ever be introduced"*), so filing a request would have asked the engine to break the architecture that decides its design. Instead a **443-byte blank A4 template** ships as an asset and New opens it through `from_bytes` — an *open*, which is the thing the shell already does well. **A4 not Letter**: Inkscape ships A4, Acrobat produces Letter only on its US branch, and this operator's corpus is A3/A1, which is A-series evidence |
| ✅ | **v0.1.0 released** — 2026-08-14, `github.com/KenM76/pdfcer-gui`, public and MIT, with a 19 MB portable Windows x64 zip that needs no install. Engine dependencies moved from relative paths to a **pinned git revision** (`718d1e9d4`), verified byte-identical to the source every binary in the session was built from, so the exe and the published source are the same engine |
| ⚠ | **★ An audit after the release found the save defect was not isolated.** Of 98 registered commands, the ones with no dispatch arm and no guard arm include **`edit.undo` / `edit.redo`** — on the QAT, bound to Ctrl+Z/Y/Shift+Z — and **every page operation**, six of which `shell::menus` offers in the Pages thumbnail context menu while `panels/pages/select.rs` maintains a full multi-select model to feed them. All inert in v0.1.0. Both are engine-supported (`can_undo`/`undo`/`redo`; `delete_pages`/`reorder_pages`/`rotate_pages`) and are being wired. **The lesson is the audit itself**: registration, a ribbon control, a chord, a context-menu row and a tooltip are five promises, and none of them is a dispatch arm |

| ✅ | **★ Undo and redo — wired 2026-08-14, after being inert for the whole project.** On the **quick access toolbar**, bound to `Ctrl+Z`, `Ctrl+Y` and `Ctrl+Shift+Z`, with no dispatch arm — the same shape as `file.save_copy`, and worse once saving worked, because every authoring feature became reachable by an operator with no way to take any of it back. Both go through `vector_edit`, so the epoch bump, the worker cancel and the texture drop come from the one funnel rather than a second mutation path. `undo.available`/`redo.available` are published at last — `app::conditions`' comment saying they were *"deliberately absent: there is no undo stack to report on yet"* is **quoted above the new code**, not deleted. A chord with an empty stack **words a decline** rather than doing nothing silently, because the greyed control is not where an operator pressing `Ctrl+Z` is looking |
| ✅ | **…and the check does not rest on counts, because a plant proved counts are not enough** — a build that mutated the session but never bumped the epoch had **every annotation count already correct** and left the canvas showing the undone state. It was caught by two oracles from *other* subsystems: `objects n=` is emitted once per `(page, epoch)` so a new line means the epoch moved, and `render-spawn` means the texture was dropped. Four plants were run and all four fired |
| ⬜ | **Undo tooltips do not name the operation** — `undo_kind()` returns the `CommandKind` and the catalog could phrase it, but `Command::tooltip` is a `String` fixed at registration and `CommandRegistry` exposes no `get_mut`; it lives in `egui-shell`. The alternative — rebuilding all 101 commands every frame for one string — also changes an icon-only control's accessible name under the pointer. Shipped with the plain label and the one-line fix recorded for whoever next opens the registry |
| ✅ | **Page operations — rotate, delete, move up/down, extract** — 2026-08-14. **Every verb the Pages context menu offers now works**; it has no inert row left. The interesting part is `resync()`, a single choke point hung off `vector_edit`'s success path rather than off the four arms — so **undo gets the page refresh for free**, because an undone delete runs the same engine command backwards through the same funnel. It compares `(page id, rotate)` per page and acts on the difference. **Rotate preserves both selections** (nothing renumbers); **reorder remaps the panel picks** through the permutation and **clears the canvas selection** (its entries carry a page *index*, and adding a remapper to `SelectionState` would put page renumbering inside the module that owns object identity); **delete clears both**. Delete discharges rule 4 with real disclosures from `DeleteOutcome` — dangling bookmarks, stale `/PageLabels`, repaired separations — observed firing on a real fixture |
| ⚠ | **★ ~~Three~~ TWO Pages commands are still live and inert on the ribbon** — `pages.split` and `pages.merge_into`. ★ **`pages.insert_from_file` shipped 2026-08-18** and is struck from this row rather than kept with a note, because the row's contract is that everything in it is inert. Neither of the two is in the context menu (the panel's own test records that exclusion as deliberate), but all three are drawn on the Pages tab and fall through to `command-unimplemented`, which is the **P3 breach** the audit was about. Each is blocked on a *decision*, not on the engine: **split** has `pageops::split`/`plan_split` but no boundary chooser, output directory or name template — and there is no honest default, since splitting a 36-sheet set into 36 files because nobody was asked is not a lesser version of the feature. **merge/insert** shared `pageops::insert`, which **returns the bytes of a new document rather than mutating the session** — so wiring it would have replaced `OpenDoc::session` wholesale and **discarded the undo log that landed the same day**. ★★ **`pages.insert_from_file` shipped 2026-08-18**, and **`merge_document` shipped in `pdfcer-core` on 2026-08-19** — one undo entry, session intact, incremental save, fields arriving fillable. So `pages.merge_into` is now unblocked and is the next Pages command to wire. ★ The way that verb came about is worth more than the verb: nobody on the engine side knew the button was parked, and it surfaced only when the shared `gui` column was re-based onto this build and **the Merge row went DOWN**. A capability marked present against a crate nobody used had been hiding a real API-shape gap. Their words: *an engine verb that exists but is the wrong shape for an editor is ours, and worth more to us than a feature request* |

| ✅ | **★ Command reachability is now checked** — `shell::commands::reach`. Every registered command must reach a dispatch arm or carry an **argued** entry in `SCAFFOLDED`; a reason under 40 characters, or one that merely restates the id, is refused, as is an entry whose command has since been wired. **38 of 101 commands have no arm**, and writing their reasons is the deliverable — it forced the question *why is this drawn at all* for each one. **Eleven are marked ★ P3**: the honest answer is that they should not be on the ribbon yet |
| ✅ | **…and the four dispatch arms for commands that do not exist are gone** — `view.zoom_in`, `view.zoom_out`, `view.next_page`, `view.prev_page`, each deleted only after **both** of its live routes were verified individually: the keyboard layer and a status-bar control. `UNREACHED_ARMS` is now **empty and kept**, its length pinned at 0, so a fifth cannot be added quietly. Three stale references fell out with them, including a `decline` test that dispatched `view.zoom_in` as *"an ordinary, unrelated verb"* — a test about any other **command** must name one that exists |

| ✅ | **Read mode and Full screen** — `Ctrl+H` and `F11` had a control, a glyph, a group and a keymap entry each, and no behaviour. ★ **Read mode is not a duplicate of the Read *mode*** — that was the question, and the answer is that they are orthogonal and compose: `mode.read` is a capability set (which tabs exist, whether the canvas authors), `view.read_mode` is a *chrome* stance (whether the ribbon and docks are drawn at all). All three reference applications separate the two, and `Ctrl+H` is Acrobat's chord for the chrome half. The **status bar stays** — it is the only remaining route to page navigation and zoom. Full screen reads its state **back off the viewport** rather than shadowing it, because a window manager can grant or revoke it unasked and a shadow would draw the control pressed over a windowed application |
| ✅ | **Render diagnostics is a report** — the status bar keeps its one-line disclosure and the dialog gets the room; both read the **same** derivation, so the editorial rules (which counters, in what order, which two excluded) are stated once |
| 🔨 | **★★★ `view.show_points` — the blocker was RE-DERIVED on 2026-08-28 and is stale.** Its recorded reason said *"this build draws no anchor mark at any rung"* and *"`nearest_node` is a query with no way to enumerate a part's anchors to paint them"*. Both are false: `canvas::overlay::draw_anchors` exists, `provider.object_node_points` and `subpath_node_points` enumerate, and `canvas::painting` already paints them — the anchors landed with the multi-node move on 2026-08-19 and the overlay's own comment says so. ★ **The dead sentence had three copies** — the register, this row, and a third — while `FEATURES.md` itself says twelve lines up *"the anchors are VISIBLE, and until 2026-08-19 they were not, at any rung."* A file contradicting itself is the shape this audit was looking for. **What is actually left is small and honest**: a `show_points` field on `ViewState`, a fourth `ViewChrome` variant, one read in the paint site — plus **one real design question**, which is that anchors draw today only for the *entered* part while the tooltip promises "every part of the object you are working inside", and `MAX_UNSELECTED_ANCHORS = 400` has already fired blank once on the operator's own SW41177 |
| ⚠ | **★ View's seven groups overflow at the shipped 1,100 px default** — found by driving. Only `page_display`, `render` and `navigate` fit; `zoom`, `display`, `panels` and **`window`** fold into the band's overflow menu, so the Read-mode and Full-screen controls just wired are reachable at that size only through the menu or their chords. The two-row change reduced this and cannot fix it — it is an **IA question**: does the View tab want splitting, or Window promoting? |

### Phase 6/S6 — rendering

| | |
|---|---|
| ⛔ | **Deep zoom via `render_page_region`** — the API shipped, but per-viewport regions cost ~700 ms each on a dense sheet. **Blocked on the reusable parsed handle**, filed in `open/request_reusable_parsed_handle.md`. Without it this trades smooth pan for zoom range |
| ⬜ | **`MAX_ZOOM` from measured performance** — not from `f32` (sub-pixel accuracy holds to ~5,000×) and not from the pixmap guard |
| ❌ | ~~Tiled rendering~~ — **cancelled.** A 1×1 *point* region costs 691 ms; a 3×3 ring is a 9× regression. See `BENCHMARK.md` |

### Standing backlog — shell-only work

Exists in `pdfcer-core` or `pdfcer`; needs a surface, not an engine.

| | |
|---|---|
| ⬜ | **★★★ Pages export as PNG, JPEG, SVG **or EMF** — 2026-09-04, `OPERATOR_REQUESTS.md` O120, BUILT AND UNDRIVEN.** ⚠ **Re-measured 2026-09-05: this row named THREE formats and there are FOUR** — `ImageFormat::ALL` is `[Png, Jpeg, Svg, Emf]`, each with its own writer, and EMF landed in the same window on the same day. A row that undercounts what shipped sends an operator looking for a command that is already on screen. The tick is also withdrawn: the window has not been driven, and ⬜ is what this file's own bar gives a capability whose only evidence is unit tests. File ▸ Export ▸ **Export image…** opens a window that asks the five questions a picture of a page actually has: **which format** (with a line under each radio saying what the receiving program will be able to do with the file), **which pages** (this one, all of them, or a typed range parsed by the print dialog's own parser — called, not copied), **what resolution** (with the pixel size of the largest page shown live, and a refusal in words if it would pass `MAX_PIXMAP_EDGE`), **whether transparency survives**, and — for JPEG only — **quality**. ★★★ **A transparent JPEG is refused BY NAME**, which is the whole of the operator's own parenthesis: *"there had better be full support (including transparency where supported!)"*. The checkbox goes **dead** when JPEG is chosen with the reason under it — the format has no way to store transparency, the page would come out on a solid background, choose PNG or SVG — and the **writer refuses the same combination a second time**, because a guard that lives only in a window makes the property *pdfcer never puts a page on white without saying so* a property of the window. ★★ **The DPI is written INTO every raster** (`pHYs` for PNG, JFIF density for JPEG) and the receipt says so rather than saying it was chosen: the engine's note names the defect — *"without `pHYs` Word places a 300 DPI page four times too large"* — and that is the difference between "we support export" and "the thing you paste is the right size". ★★★ **Every export carries a Rule-4 disclosure, off-canvas and afterwards**: for SVG, `ExportTally`'s counters in English — shadings embedded as pictures, soft masks Inkscape honours and Word ignores, overprint and non-separable blends drawn as their `Normal` approximation, dashed strokes pre-applied, blend modes Word's importer drops — plus, **always and unconditionally, that text became glyph outlines**, which nothing counts and which no inspection of the file would reveal. An exact page **says it was exact**, because a silent disclosure and a broken one look identical. ★ **A revision in the filename survives**: `set_extension` on a `plan.rev2` stem eats the revision, so `plan.rev2.pdf` and `plan.rev3.pdf` would both have suggested `plan.png` and the second would overwrite the first — caught by its own test on the first run, and the DXF export's comment claims the opposite behaviour and is wrong about it. ★ **Several pages become one save dialog and a stem** (`drawing-p1.png`, `drawing-p2.png`…), Acrobat's own convention, stated in the window **before** the picker opens because a save dialog cannot say *"the name you type is a stem"*. ⚠ **NOT DRIVEN.** The window has never been opened in a running binary: the operator was at his keyboard and the harness steals the screen. Eight pure assertions cover the refusal, the receipt, the tally-to-sentence mapping, the range parse, the filename derivation and the 72-per-inch scale, and **every one was falsified** — but by this project's own bar a row is ticked when an operator can reach it in a real build, so this one is ✅ for *built and tested* and the driven check is outstanding |
| ✅ | **★★★ The Attachments panel — 2026-08-28. A PDF can carry whole files inside itself, and this shell had no way to reach any of them.** `attach_file`, `detach_file`, `list_attachments_with_notes`, `extract_attachment` and `sanitize_attachment_name` had all shipped in `pdfcer-core`, with fixtures, a hazard analysis and a spec-ambiguity register — and there was no command, no menu item and no panel. An engine capability with no operator surface is, from the operator's chair, a capability that does not exist. Now: **Edit ▸ Insert ▸ Attachments** (`edit.attachments`) opens a list of what the document carries — name, description, size, dates, media type, and which of the two mechanisms holds it — with **Attach file…** above the list and per-row **Save a copy…** and **Remove**. ★★ **The shape is Acrobat's**, per the standing rule: a list with an add control above it, a save and a delete per row. ★★ **`RIBBON_IA.md` names attachments nowhere** — not in §5.2's Panels row, not in §5.1's Document band, not anywhere — so the tab is argued rather than read off, and the argument is `Panel::Redact`'s: Read is shown `file` and `view` alone, and a surface that embeds and removes whole files must not be reachable from a reading stance. The cost is stated rather than hidden — Acrobat *Reader* lists attachments and saves them out, and this build's Read mode cannot; the fix that day is a second, verb-less panel, not a second id for this one. ★★★ **Three disclosures the engine makes mandatory in its own doc comments**, all of them off-canvas because none of this is on any page: **removing does not erase** (under the default incremental save §7.5.6 every prior revision stays in the file, so the bytes are recoverable until a full rewrite — the sentence names *save a compacted copy* as the remedy, because a warning with no route out is half a disclosure); **the bytes may be ciphertext** (since PDF 1.5 an embedded file can be encrypted in an otherwise *unencrypted* document via `/EFF`, so the intuitive guard fails **silently** and a successful-looking extraction yields garbage); and **the name written to disk may not be the name shown** (the listing reports the raw name because a reader that quietly repairs its evidence is not a reader, and the filesystem gets `sanitize_attachment_name`'s, with the hazards translated into English rather than printed as Rust identifiers). ★ **Three absences, each R9 rather than an omission**: no *Edit description* (`attach_file` takes it at attach time and no verb changes one afterwards — so no control, and the *limit* is disclosed in the attach row); no *Remove* on a page-level row (`detach_file` answers `AttachmentNotFound` for a `/FileAttachment` **by name**, so those are removed as annotations, and the row says so); no *Open* (the bytes came from inside a file that arrived from somewhere, and `pdfcer-core` is explicit that it does not execute or interpret them). ★ **One refusal is surfaced and three are not**: `AttachmentTreeUnsupported` — a multi-node `/Kids` name tree the engine will not risk repairing — is unreachable from any other surface and otherwise produces *nothing at all* on the press, which is indistinguishable from a broken button; `DocumentEncrypted`, the certification gate and `ObjectCreationWouldExposeHiddenObjects` take this shell's settled answer and reach the trace |
| ✅ | **A text tool for Edit** — 2026-08-14. `CanvasTool::Text`, armed by `view.tool_text` beside the hand tool in View ▸ Navigate. **The reference applications disagreed and the disagreement is the interesting part**: Acrobat and SolidWorks resolve text-versus-object *contextually inside one tool*; Inkscape uses a separate Text tool. Inkscape won, and **not on a head-count** — an object marquee over vector content is a surface Acrobat does not have at all, so its contextual answer is not an answer to this conflict. The deciding argument is concrete: a contextual press would make a marquee over a region containing text unpredictable, and that is the commonest gesture in Edit. The variant carries **nothing** where `Markup` and `Measure` each carry a kind — the only thing text needs to persist is that it is armed. It requires **no capability**: retiring it on entering Read would take away a tool that mode plainly permits, and retiring it on entering Edit would delete the feature on the frame the operator enters the mode it exists for |
| 🔨 | **★★ Annotations are SELECTABLE — 2026-08-18, and it was the answer to four operator reports at once.** *"How do I edit a stamp I've applied?"* · *"I still can't get to edit dimension groups when I click on it"* · *"I click and can't figure out how to enable some of the basic stuff"* · *"it feels like nothing is moving forward on these things."* One cause, recorded in the row below on 2026-08-17 and ending *"The second is ours"*: `Selection` named a paint-order index into page **content**, so a stamp, note, shape or ce dimension was not selectable at all — and could not be **deleted** except by `Ctrl+Z` immediately after placing. Now: click one in Review or Edit, it outlines; click away, it clears; `Delete` or Format ▸ Delete removes it through the same `vector_edit` funnel as every other edit, so it undoes and invalidates caches the one way that happens everywhere. ★ **Two predicates were too coarse and neither was a broken mechanism** — the click in Review was consumed by `textsel::takes_the_press` (true for the Select tool whenever `edit_content` is false, i.e. in the mode an operator is in *because* they are marking up), and the Delete key returned on `!edit_content` above any annotation branch. Now one predicate per capability: `author_markup` guards the annotation verb, `edit_content` the content verb, and entering Review from Edit drops a selected path while **keeping** a selected stamp. ★ **My first diagnosis was wrong and an existing test refused it** — I widened `press_kind`'s click predicate believing Review produced no click event; it produces one, and `review_mode_places_markup_but_refuses_content` failed against the change. The routing had no arm, not the predicate. `AnnotKind` carries Markup vs CeDimension **in the type** so the Format tab's routing to `set_dimension_style` is a match the compiler checks — restyling a ce dimension as ordinary markup regenerates it as a bare line with its label and witness lines gone. **No grips on the outline**: they would promise a resize `set_markup_style` deliberately excludes. Still missing and stated: **move, resize and restyle** — that is the Format tab slice, next |
| ✅ | **★★★ A placed form field's properties are editable — 2026-08-27, and the pane had been telling the operator to delete their field.** Click a field and the Properties pane offers **Required**, **Read only** and a **tooltip** for every type, plus **multiple lines**, **hide as typed**, **equal cells** and a **maximum length** on a text field, **cannot be cleared** on a radio group, and **drop-down** / **allow several** on a choice. One press, one `EditSession::edit_field`, one undo entry. ★★★ **The sentence this deletes was false the day it was written**: *"Required, read-only, the tooltip and the border can only be set when a field is placed. To change one, delete this field and place a new one."* `edit_field` and `edit_widget` landed as `Pass 134.0` on **2026-08-26** — the same day — three commits before the revision this shell compiles against, and the engine wrote a **96-line pane design brief** into the request channel saying so. Nothing consumed it, so for a day the program recommended a **destructive workaround** — delete-and-replace loses the field's name, its filled value and its place in the tab order, all three of which an FDF import or a filling script keys on. ★★ **The scope rule shapes the whole pane and is taken verbatim from Acrobat's scripting model**: some properties *"apply to all widgets that are children of that field"* and others *"are specific to individual widgets"*. `edit_field` writes the first group once and every placement follows — so `widgets_affected > 1` is disclosed, because the operator is looking at one box and three just changed. The widget-scoped four (rect, border, visibility, caption) are `edit_widget` and are the next piece of work, named in the pane rather than left as a silence. ★★ **Three disclosures Acrobat performs silently** are surfaced verbatim: a `/MaxLen` shortened below the current value, a selected choice option removed (Acrobat re-points the selection **by numeric index**, so it can land on a *different* option), and a check box's export value changed while checked (it renders **unchecked**). pdfcer neither refuses nor repairs — truncating the operator's data or re-pointing their selection would be inventing document state — and `value_no_longer_fits` is a ready-made sentence passed through unreworded. ★ **Comb sends two properties in one edit**, and it is not a batch: Table 228 permits `Comb` only when `/MaxLen` is present and the engine checks its gates **against the resulting field**, so it is one act the standard makes indivisible. ★ **No Type control, and nothing greyed where one would be** — Acrobat has offered no field-type conversion since Acrobat 6, and pdfcer models the limit by making the request *unrepresentable*, so there is nothing to disable. ★★ **DRIVEN** — `form_field` extended: place a field, click it, scroll to the properties, tick Required, and `edit-field` reaches the document; **falsified** by cutting the section out and rebuilding, which reports the operator's own state back. Driving found two things unit tests could not: the section sits **below the fold** of the Properties slot (two scroll notches), and a section rect published through `ui_rect_visible` **vanishes when the section is taller than its slot** — filed to `D:/dev/rag/egui/` |
| ✅ | **★★ Flatten works from the ribbon — 2026-08-27, and it had been drawn and inert for the whole life of the project.** `edit.form_flatten` sat on Edit ▸ Forms with an icon and an enable predicate and **no dispatch arm**, while the Forms panel's own Flatten button called `flatten_fields` perfectly well. What kept it inert was a `SCAFFOLDED` entry whose stated reason had become false in **both halves** — *"unbuilt"* (it cites a `FEATURES.md` row that was itself stale) and *"irreversible on the document"* (it is one `EditSession` command and one `Ctrl+Z`, and the shell's own tooltip copy had already argued at length that flatten appends an overlay and leaves existing content byte-verbatim). ★ **A citation of a citation, and nothing re-read either.** It is the **fifth** stale blocker this project has found and the fourth was its neighbour, a day earlier. The staleness test cannot catch this class: it asks whether an id has an arm, and an entry with no arm and a nonsense *reason* is indistinguishable from a correct one. A reader is the only instrument, and the rule that came out of it is written on the assertion: **when you touch that list for any purpose, re-derive the reason of the entry beside the one you came for.** The arm asks `flatten_refusal` — the **strict** structural gate, not `fill_refusal` — because on a certified fillable form at `/P 2` filling is permitted and flattening is refused, and an operator who has just typed into the form needs to be told which rule stopped them |
| ✅ | **★★★ The Format tab's Font group, and three surfaces that say how to reach it — 2026-08-27.** *"Make the text tool discoverable, then the Format tab's Font group."* Sweep text and the contextual **Format** tab now carries a **Font** band — face, size, Bold, Italic, colour — the same five controls as the Properties panel and, by `RIBBON_IA.md` §5.8's build order, deliberately **no more**: Grow and Shrink were refused because a control that exists only on the tab is the "writing the editors twice" the build order exists to prevent. ★★★ **The discoverability half is the harder one and it is what the operator actually asked for.** O37 shipped with an admission in its own row — *you must press `T` before sweeping, and nothing on screen says so* — and three surfaces now do: **(1)** clicking a piece of text raises the Format tab with the Font group **greyed**, and R9 permits greying only when it explains itself, so every one of the five tooltips names the route and reads correctly in both states (`render_command` shows the same string through `on_hover_text` and `on_disabled_hover_text`); **(2)** the Properties panel draws *This text* with one sentence naming the tool and the key — ★ which its own module header had **claimed existed for a week and did not**, because `section` returned before drawing anything whenever there was no sweep, so the panel said nothing in exactly the state the paragraph described; **(3)** the Tool panel's text row names the sweep, where it had described two of the tool's three uses and left out the one the operator was looking for. ★★ **Two conditions, two rules, and getting either wrong is a shipped defect.** `mode.edit_content` is each item's `visible_when` so the band is **absent** in Read and Review (a capability a mode does not have renders nothing); `selection.text` is each command's `enabled_when` so inside Edit it **greys** until something is swept (an operand that the next gesture supplies is temporarily unavailable). One condition alone would either draw an enabled Bold in Read that the mode gate must refuse, or an enabled Bold in Edit that does nothing on almost every press. ★ **The tab's own condition changed** from `selection.any` to `selection.formattable` — the union — because the tab now has two kinds of subject and neither operand is its question; spelled either way round it loses one of them, and the `SELECTION_ANY` alias was de-aliased first so that editing one could not silently retarget the canvas right-click menu's Delete. ★ **Three of the five controls are `Item::Custom`s** — a face chooser, a size field, a colour swatch — drawn by `app::fontband`, which does the four things the shell does for a command item and cannot do for a custom one: evaluate the predicate, grey, show the tooltip, and publish the rect under `ribbon.item.<id>` so a driven check finds a combo the same way it finds a button. `egui_shell::manifest::Item::Custom` gained a `visible_when` for this, which is what makes the whole band vanish in Read rather than leaving three holes in a slot the plan had already reserved |
| ✅ | **★★★ Restyling EXISTING text — 2026-08-27, and O37's own inventory said it was impossible.** *"We should also have all the font tools available that Word does."* Sweep text on the page (press **T** to arm the text tool, then drag) and the Properties panel grows a **This text** section: font, size, **Bold**, **Italic**, colour. Every control is one `EditSession::format_text` and one undo entry. ★★ **The fourteen-row table this request opened with was wrong in every cell of its "on EXISTING text" column.** `format_text` had shipped as `Pass 14.2` three weeks earlier, been extended through `19.x`, and become form-retargetable as `Pass 119.2` five days BEFORE the request that said it did not exist — it had reached this project only as a paragraph inside a note about something else. Half the lesson is ours: **an absence claim about a crate you do not build is a claim about EVERY route**, and that inventory checked one verb. ★★★ **Bold is never greyed and one press can take either of two verbs.** `set_font` selects a real face and refuses when the page has none; `gate_synthesis` refuses synthesis when a real face exists. ~~Exact complements, so every page is covered.~~ ★ **RETRACTED 2026-08-27 by the engine, in writing, after they reproduced it** — they are not complements, and the counter-example is the next sentence. The buttons still do not grey, because greying them would mean predicting a refusal that turns on a per-run glyph-coverage test this shell cannot run without doing the engine's work. The button asks for synthesis and, when refused *because a real face is available*, retries with the face the refusal names. ★★★ **That retry found an engine defect**: on `textedit/format_family.pdf` the gate names `Times-Bold` (family-matching the run) and `Times-Bold` remaps `o` to a bullet, so it cannot cover the text while `Calibri-Bold` on the same page can — bold is unreachable there through either verb. Filed, **not worked around**: a shell-side search for a different bold resource is twenty lines and is this project second-guessing pdfcer's font selection. ★★ **DRIVEN**, against the operator's own SW41177 title block — 19 show operators restyled across 14 runs in 1.1 s — and **falsified in the same session**: with the panel section cut out and the binary rebuilt, the check reports his own symptom back. Driving found **four defects the eight unit tests could not**, the first being that a text *run* is not a show *operator*. **Not here, said rather than hidden:** one sweep over N pieces is N undo entries — `EditSession` has no grouping verb, so the count is disclosed rather than left to be found; and a CMYK or spot-colour run shows a sentence rather than a swatch, because changing it here would convert the ink permanently. The first two entries on this list — the missing Font group and the silence on a clicked text object — **shipped the same day**, and have their own row below |
| ⛔ | **★ The Format contextual tab — measured 2026-08-17 and BLOCKED, not merely unbuilt.** `RIBBON_IA.md` §5.8 calls it *"the single largest usability change proposed here … what makes selection **mean** something"* and specifies twenty-four property editors across six selection types. It can carry **one** of them, `Delete`, which it already has. Two independent blockers, neither schedulable away. **(1) `EditSession` has no verb that modifies an annotation** — `add_markup`, `add_text_annotation`, `delete_annotation` and two deletion predicates is the whole surface, so a markup's colour, width, fill, opacity, arrowheads and note text cannot be changed after placement. Delete-and-re-add is not a workaround and is deliberately not built: it loses the annotation's object identity and with it its `/NM`, its z-order in `/Annots`, and any reply thread hung off it as an `/IRT` target. The one exception is the **ce dimension** row — `set_group_style`, `set_dimension_style`, `set_group_scale`, `set_group_standard` all exist, so dimensions have a style model and nothing else does. **(2) The canvas selection cannot address an annotation** — `Selection` is `page + object + subpath + node`, four integers naming a paint-order index into page *content*, which is what makes it immune to zoom and also means a markup or dimension is not selectable at all. The second is ours; the first is filed. `manifest/format.rs`'s header carries the measurement. ★ **BOTH halves are now discharged** — blocker (1) cleared 2026-08-18 with `set_markup_style`, blocker (2) the same day with the row above. What remains is building the tab, which is work rather than a block |
| ✅ | **★ New at a chosen page size — 2026-08-18, and it was an ENGINE gap wearing a GUI gap's clothes.** `NO_SURFACE.md` listed *"new blank page size — A4, baked-in template, none"* as an unbuilt shell surface. It was not: nothing in `pdfcer-core` wrote a `/MediaBox`, so the only implementation available to a shell was **one checked-in template asset per size** — ten with landscape, more with ANSI, **and a custom size impossible at any count**. That half-capability was refused rather than built, on the argument that a surface answering twelve of thirteen cases forecloses the fix for the thirteenth, and the gap was filed instead. `EditSession::set_media_box` and `pdfcer_core::paper` shipped the next day. `file.new_from_template` now offers **A0–A6, Letter, Legal, Tabloid, Executive, ANSI A–E**, both orientations, and a custom size in millimetres bounded by ISO 32000-1 Annex C.2's advised 14,400 units — advice, and labelled as such, since 32000-2 drops the number entirely. The A series is listed **largest-first**, which is the engine's own ordering and is deliberate: this operator's sheets are A1 and A3. ★ The document is serialized and **re-parsed** rather than handed over as an edited session, so a brand-new A1 sheet is not already modified with `Ctrl+Z` waiting to take it back to A4. `file.new` is unchanged — Ctrl+N still makes A4 and asks nothing, which is Inkscape's split and what two of the three reference applications do. Driven by `new_document_sizes_the_page`, which picks A3, clicks Landscape, and reads the page back **after the re-parse** — the four places a size can be lost each look correct from the one next to it, and the unit tests can only see the first two |
| ⬜ **BUILT AND UNDRIVEN** | **★★★ An open drawing's sheets can be put on a different size of paper — 2026-09-06, and the capability had been sitting in the engine, unreachable, for nineteen days.** `pages.resize` on Pages ▸ Transform, beside Rotate. Pick sheets in the Pages panel or none at all — the same operand rule every other `pages.*` command uses, picked sheets else the current one — press **Sheet size…**, choose from A0–A6, Letter, Legal, Tabloid, Executive, ANSI A–E or a custom size in millimetres, and every picked sheet changes as **one undo entry**. ★★★ **The real product of the window is a MEASUREMENT, not a size list.** Changing a `/MediaBox` changes the **paper**; it does not move, scale or reflow one byte of what is drawn on the page — so an A1 drawing put on A4 paper is **cropped, not shrunk**, and every other page-size control the operator has met (Word, LibreOffice, a print dialog's Fit-to-page) reflows or scales. Measured 2026-09-06 through the engine's own `set-page-size` on `fixtures/a1-titleblock.pdf`, A1 → A4: every glyph run's coordinate is **byte-identical**, and the title block — x 1831–2207 pt — is entirely off a sheet that stops at 595.28. Annotation `/Rect`s, form fields and ce dimensions are identical too (a ce dimension's printed value stayed `400.00 pt`, which is *correct*: the drawing did not move). `/CropBox`, `/BleedBox`, `/TrimBox` and `/ArtBox` are left byte-identical; only `/MediaBox` is rewritten. ⇒ So the window states the rule, **draws the old sheet, the new sheet and the drawing's own extent to scale**, and says in points how far the drawing would run past the paper being chosen — *“the drawing runs 1,636 pt past the right edge”* — recomputed as the size changes, **before anything is committed**. ★★ That overhang is a thing the ENGINE says it cannot report: `MediaBoxChange::lost_area`'s named residual is that it knows the *sheet* shrank and not whether any *content* was in the region it lost, *“because pdfcer has no page-content bounding-box facility”*. True of `EditSession` and false of `pdfcer-core` — `PageObjects::page_bbox` is exactly that, and this shell already holds one per page. Its boundary is stated on screen rather than only in a doc comment: comments, form fields and ce dimensions keep their positions too and are **not** counted in that measurement. ★ It also opens on **what the sheets already are**, not on A4, so an operator who opens it to look is not one press from cropping an A1 drawing; and the new sheet keeps the corner the old ones had rather than being forced to the origin, which matters on imposition output and cropped scans. ⚠ **No scale-to-fit, deliberately.** The engine has no such verb; composing one would be six verbs across N pages, and its most likely failure — a ce dimension group left at the old calibration — prints a wrong measurement and looks perfectly correct. `app::actions::pagesize`'s header carries the full argument. ⬜ The driven check `resizing_a_sheet_changes_the_paper_in_the_saved_file` is written, compiles and is **NOT REGISTERED**: another track owns `checks/mod.rs` and `roster.rs`. It takes its verdict in a **second process** — a fresh binary opens the saved file and reports the page sizes it resolves from the bytes — with page 1 as a negative control in the same trace line family in the same run |
| ✅ | **★ A crosshair pdfcer draws itself — 2026-08-18, operator report.** *"The crosshairs when over the canvas are white making it hard to see them."* Nothing in this crate drew them: `CursorIcon::Crosshair` resolves to Windows' `IDC_CROSS`, a **monochrome** cursor whose colour belongs to the operator's pointer scheme and to the accessibility pointer-colour setting — neither readable nor influenceable by an application. So pdfcer stops asking for it and hands the OS its own bitmap through `set_cursor_image`, which is a **real OS cursor**: no lag, not clipped by our window, unlike a `Painter`-drawn one. ★ **Two tones, not the inversion the report assumed** — XOR cursors were an AND/XOR mask pair and compositing killed them, since no blend mode means *"invert what is beneath me"*.
